ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e2734	Mr President. Mr Federal Minister, my question relates to your area of employment, which concerns unemployment. The request submitted in writing has the following wording: [[Quoted print version removed]]
ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e2766	Before the Minister answers: I must treat all Members equally, and every Member of this House needs to explain his question briefly. Dr. Khol: Whoever still does not know, loses the right to question!]] Mr Minister, I would like a reply.
ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e2795	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! The topic of long-term unemployment is one of the most sensitive issues of labour market policy. Together we want to reduce long-term unemployment as much as possible. It is one of the main objectives of an active labour market policy, the labour market policy of the AMS to do so. We all know that, mainly due to the relatively dramatic economic downturn, the development in the labour market in recent months has been absolutely unacceptable, but long-term unemployment has, of course, declined in the last two years. The concrete figures: Since February 2000, i.e. since the start of government, the number of long-term unemployed has fallen from almost 24 000 to almost 12 000, i.e. more than 50 percent, by 51.4 percent. In the annual comparison of December 2000 to December 2001, this decline is still 21.1 percent. The long-term unemployed, who are unemployed for more than six months, are less strong, but still pleasant: minus 20.5 percent since the beginning of 2000 and minus 12.6 percent since December 2000 in the annual period. The very important point in this context is also how long unemployed are on average unemployed, how fast the AMS communicates how quickly unemployed people find a new job. The average length of stay in unemployment has fallen in the two-year period, from 98 days in December 1999, ladies and gentlemen, on 86 days in December 2001, that is, less 12 days, which is also very pleasing.

Unfortunately, it took a very long time in the municipality of Vienna and it was particularly difficult to launch the project "Integra". Today, it is one of many established instruments to combat long-term unemployment, and I say that the central importance here is the special recruitment aid. This is an instrument that provides active wage support when enterprises recruit long-term unemployed. I consider this to be the much more useful instrument compared to what is now being discussed in Germany. In what is going on in Germany under the slogan "Combination", long-term unemployed people are to be put into work through supported social security contributions, and even the government-leading group in Germany says that they hope to get 30 000 unemployed away. In Austria, this would be minus 3,000 – we would rather stay with our established instruments. In this I am perfectly of one opinion with the leadership of the AMS.

Unfortunately, in the 1980s in Austria this was God’s result from the nationalized industry. There it became quite modern, personnel problems, earnings problems with the termination, with the release of older workers to "complete" – I deliberately put it under quotation marks – and in Austria it became a mainstream, the Golden Handshake. Often, very successful press conferences were held, while at the same time the termination of older workers – mostly agreeable, mostly with Golden Handshake – was communicated. This trend is turning slowly, because employers, people in this country, know that we need to have older workers in the labour market in the long term, apart from short-term economic fluctuations, especially in the labour market. They will be needed again on the basis of their experience, due to their expertise in all areas of this country's working life.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e3142	The next question is Mr Schwemmlein, and I should like to ask him to formulate it.
ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e3157	Federal Minister! My question is: [[/PAKT/VHG/XXI/M/M_00146/index.shtml -- 146/M]] What steps does the Government take up to the planned in-force step of the Basel II agreement on own resources rules for credit institutions in 2005 to discharge small and medium-sized enterprises?
ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e3184	Mr. Federal Minister, please answer.
ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e3200	Ladies and gentlemen, I should like to thank the honourable Member, Mrs Schwemmlein, for the excellent work he has done. Basel II has been with us and elsewhere for a few months, especially in Europe. It is about the Bank for International Balance of Payments in Basel. Austria is not a member of the Basel II Committee – this has historical reasons, it cannot be changed in the short term – but some EU countries and, for example, Switzerland are members there. The Basel II Committee is considering whether this general rule that loans from banks with 8 percent own resources should not be weighed by the risk of these loans. In principle, there is nothing contrary to this, why should this not happen? However, we demand that this be done in a way that allows the medium-sized economy to live, and in a way that does not allow large American banks to implement their ideas and ultimately their practice, but which takes account of European realities. In Austria, for example, the financing of enterprises is 65 percent through loans – the rest are equity and equity replacements. In the U.S., companies are financed only by 20 percent from loans. So the problem is three and a half times as big as in the United States. At the margins of the World Economic Forum in New York, I had the opportunity to talk to the number two of the American currency system, William McDonough, President of the Federal Reserve Bank of New York. At the same time, he is the chairman of the Basel II Committee and has explained to me that it is also his interest to ultimately make Basel II as medium-sized as possible. He also knows that today, new jobs, innovation, research and development are mostly involved in small and medium-sized enterprises and not in the very large ones. Concrete: What do we ask Austrians to do about our ways, in the end also through the European Commission, because this must be carried into our law through an EU directive? – We want the loans for small and medium-sized medium-sized enterprises to fall under the Basel II limit, which is to be treated equally with private loans, with retail credits. This seems to be possible because the banker in Austria knows the entrepreneur, the entrepreneur finally ad personam. 5 million € are mine here, our wish limit. If this succeeds – that is by no means certain, but you have to set goals – then far, well over 90 percent of medium-sized companies are outside because they do not have larger loans. Secondly, long-term loans are not to be ponised. This is totally contrary to our view. We have always learned: Investments are financed by long-term, in no case short-term loans. Basel II, in its first statement, says that long-term loans have to be penalised by laying down rules. Thirdly, it is a question of keeping things safe, mortgages and the like, as credit-guarantee opportunities. These are the three most important things that we – the Chamber of Commerce, the social partners, the National Bank, also the banking sector in Austria – want very close, and it looks pretty good. The third consultation paper will be postponed once at the end of 2002 to give way to negotiations. They said "2005" – it could certainly be 2006.

If the entrepreneur wants to get the money out of the company, then it remains with the previous status quo, i.e. 50 percent tax. That's not so little.

The Austrian Parliament takes into account the Bologna Declaration, which aims to create a European Higher Education Area. We improve the transfer possibilities between universities and universities. At the same time, however, I would like to point out that it is not the goal of this being done in a consecutive sequence – that is, Bachelor, Master, Doctorate – but rather that it would be very useful in the sense of life-long learning to divide these phases into later stages, that is, that professional phases are between them. High house! ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e8967	Ms Wolfmayr has been reported as the next speaker. Voluntary speaking time limit: 6 minutes. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e8988	Mr President. Mrs. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! I do not want to go into the distractions of my otherwise appreciated colleague Niederwieser now. I would like to return to the subject and talk about it. Environmental changes and their growing problems require solutions based on interdisciplinary considerations. Economic, social, legal and philosophical aspects must be taken into account so that the systemic, nonlinear, highly networked nature of environmental systems can be matched. The basic idea of a diploma degree in environmental systems sciences is to learn the basics of other disciplines and establish relationships between them in addition to well-founded technical training. Today, the present application is intended to amend the university study law to include the degree in environmental system sciences in the regular course of study. Ladies and gentlemen! Short to the history: Since the winter semester 1992/1993, environmental system science has been offered as an individual degree at the University of Graz, my home university. A well-founded training in business, economics, chemistry, physics and geography – depending on selection at the moment – is to be combined with a freely-electable set of compulsorys and systems sciences. The students thus acquire the basics and the thinking of some further disciplines. This type of study has proved its worth and is therefore intended to be offered as a regular course of study. I am glad about it and expressly welcome it. Ladies and gentlemen! The study plan for the study of the environmental system sciences clearly shows the benefits and possibilities of a contemporary, necessary, modern, networked study of the students themselves, but ultimately and subsequently all of us, the whole society, bring to the environment. Some advantages: Through joint courses – for example, mathematics, statistics, system analysis, ecology – as well as interdisciplinary internships, the editing of environmental issues is learned through team work. Relationships between different sciences will be evident, interactions can be better observed and assessed. The development of environmentally friendly products and services is always at the heart of the future. The graduates are able, after completing their studies, to work in environmental research and teaching, in support and advice of environmental protection institutions or in project management, as well as in areas of business advice and management, where environmental conditions need to be taken into account. Thank God more and more such companies. In short, a document I have received for information: In the summer of 1998, a questionnaire was sent out – my knowledge does not have a more up-to-date questionnaire and no more up-to-date evaluation – and the return rate was at least 68 percent. However, this questionnaire and its results document the positive development and upward trend of this study, which has continued since then. Students were asked, on the one hand, about their careers from graduation and about their current primary activity. Some graduates carried out projects on a contractual basis for public institutions before entering a fixed service relationship. Others are involved in environmental planning, environmental management, ecodesign, other teaching at university institutions and other training opportunities or research in public and private institutions. More than 70 percent already found a job over contacts they made during the course of their studies. It seems remarkable to me that environmental scientists trained in job vacancies are already specifically addressed. Main reasons for students to choose this study are summarised: Interdisciplinarity, the alternative to conventional study institutions, the possibility of partly independent design of their study plan and, last but not least, good professional prospects. Again: This bill is to be welcomed, as well as the consent of all parties to this request, which brings an end to a provisional state and leads a degree to a regular degree. Thank you.

I know you've been talking about 250 times, maybe it was even 300 times, but when conversations actually lead to nothing but hardened fronts and that the people concerned are getting more and more affected because of this conversation climate, that can't be good, even if you say you're talking to the people concerned. I say now with all the hardness: If the Federal Government, if the Ministry submits a 85-page discussion plan that takes into account the medical universities in 19 lines in such a way that everyone shouts and says, if that comes over us, we cannot survive, because this goes back to the state of law of the last decades, where special provisions for medicine were formulated for good reason, then it is dangerous. And if I hear today, Well, if the doctors are so excited that we only write in the medical universities as an option, then we do it differently, then the faculties stay at the university as a whole and the students also stay at the university as a whole, but there are no special provisions, I mean: The universities and the authorities involved are not delinquents, which are then exempted, decapitated or quadrupled. I think that this is grossly negligent and not a sign of discussion. [[Congratulation with the Greens and the Abg. Dr. Niederwieser.]] I say again: All the rectors, the Austrian rectors' conference, the three rectors of the affected sites, the Senate of the affected sites, the Innsbruck Faculty College, even the Innsbruck Municipal Council under van Staa have said: No, please don't! The platform, even though it may not vote – it may already be, but it may think and say something – has expressed itself unanimously, in order not to say unanimously, against this exclusion. We are concerned that our own medical universities are difficult to test on Wolfmayr's well-tailed interdisciplinary demands for transdisciplinarity, which make the dependence on the carrier and holding company stronger than it has been until now. And I'll give you something to consider: Some homework has also been missed in the last decades. There are huge sums of money that go to medicine because a good part of the work of the staff there is only and alone in the care of the sick. And this provision of the sick is legally attributed to the countries, not the Minister of State. In other words, money is partially used for the benefit of our beloved federalism. Since 1956, there has been a regulation that 18 percent of the total cost of the university clinics is recovered by the Republic as a so-called clinical additional burden, based on services for research and teaching at these clinics. Since 1956, there has been no regulation, no decree on how to calculate this and how such payment arrangements would be restrictively compatible with the institution. There is no cooperation agreement between the Republic of Austria and the agencies responsible for providing health care. And now, if the medical university existed, it would do what we did not do for 30 years. I'll tell you something. Of course, carriers and countries – which is legitimate and logical – have an interest in primarily getting to grips with health care and offering only secondary studies, only secondaryly looking at the fact that you are at the top of international research, because that is not the task of the countries either. If we are now suspended from this parallelogram, the distribution of weapons – if I may use this ungood military expression, but which is appropriate here – does not look favourable to the Republic and not favourable to the medical universities. [[Congratulations with the Greens and representatives of the SPÖ.]] And last but not least: If all these voices don't do anything and we're saying we're standing up for an example without knowing these consequences, I'm really looking for you now – I'm even asking you to do it and don't enjoy it at all – to resume this discussion process before something happens, which is difficult to correct. Another thing: If individuals want to release the medicine from the university, I call it a reason that is not very high and not very holy. At the medical university clinics and also at individual theoretical institutes a pretty sum of money is collected, a very large sum, under the title "Research on behalf of third parties" or "Diagnostics on behalf of third parties" or "treatment of private patients". That's billions over the years. Some deserve it very well, so I don't want to call you the numbers in order not to harm our reputation any more. But these have the greatest interest in not being controlled by a rector of the University, not to come into contact with theology, philosophy, sociology and psychology, because one is very reluctant to look at these cards. You have written a letter to all the rectors and deans, including on my suggestion – I am grateful to you – in which you carried out how much of this revenue should be carried out. The revenue, I think, has increased by 0.8 percent on this warning letter in Innsbruck. So you can see how much money you can set free for good purposes if you support medicine in such a way that it is not subject to a dictatorship, but to meaningful control and cooperation with the University as a whole. I would ask you once again to take up this debate.

In particular, the submission provides for criminalisation of the financing of criminal offences, whereby the Parties undertake to punish such acts domestically and to take measures to freeze or confiscate money intended to carry out terrorist crimes. In principle, the Convention imposes penalties on the financing and instigation of or participation in the financing of terrorist acts. But it is all to be said that in the future this will no longer be necessary for military operations, as they have unfortunately been all too often, to cease to happen in the future, that they are finally in the past, because the international community has developed mechanisms that prevent such networks from developing. I believe that the implementation of this agreement will create instruments to prevent networks from developing internationally, in particular by removing funding opportunities. There are now possibilities for this, and in this area, in particular, an intensification of criminal law also makes a completely different apprehension than before. A large number of preparatory work has already been carried out in Austria. It will implement all that is planned internationally. This government makes a significant contribution to the fact that international terrorist networks, as they have now existed, will no longer be able to exist, in particular by hopefully withdrawing the funding base from them.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e10405	Mr President. Mrs. Federal Minister! I think today's debate is marked by all our conviction that terrorism must be combated in all its forms. I believe that it is important to make a political statement and also to include political phenomena that have become so up-to-date in recent days, because Iraq is a state – and this is included in the assessment – which is based on UN sanctions. Before I begin my contribution to the current item on the agenda, I would like to ask Mrs Bundesminister a very specific question, namely: Mr. Dr. Haider said yesterday in the "time in the picture" at 1 p.m. – I quote Dr. Haider literally ; I have to quote this here –: [[Abg. Dr. Ofner:... I'm just defending you! I have to put my defense back!]] "In the meantime, I can also tell you that neither the United Nations nor the Austrian Government sees a violation of the UN embargo in this action. I called the Federal Chancellor last night, who said: Please, say that at the press conference, there are no interventions by the UN here, and there is no reason to intervene from the Austrian side either." That is also my question to you, Mrs Federal Minister. I would now like to deal with the International Convention on the Control of the Financing of Terrorism. Terrorism has primarily a political cause, and therefore it can not only be a military fight against terrorism, but also, of course, a political solution to the causes of terrorism, which must on the one hand be presented in a higher distribution justice and on the other hand in sufficient future visions for the individual regions and peoples, including the social responsibility of the European nations, industrial nations. The agreement we are discussing today and which we agree with is intended to provide the basis for the technical implementation of the fight against terrorism. The Austrian Social Democratic Party, particularly after the terrible events of the previous year, supports the fight against international terrorism and, therefore, the present International Convention against the Financing of Terrorism. However, I would like to point out that one must be aware that a greater degree of security and control means a reduction of another good, namely personal freedom in privacy. It is therefore important to find the right balance between these two objectives. Although this International Convention in Austria does not directly produce legal effects – it must be fulfilled by the adoption of laws at national level – I believe that it is in a number of other legal provisions, such as the UN Resolution and the EU Regulation; and the latter is directly applicable law in Austria. Accounts and financial resources related to terrorism can now be immediately blocked and frozen on this legal basis. The International Convention against the Financing of Terrorism requires the establishment of measures and the adaptation of national legislation, as has already been said. I would like to point out a number of areas where changes need to be made: Article 18(1) 1 of the Convention calls for the creation of a new criminal record in the Criminal Code with regard to the intentional financing of the preparation of terrorist crimes and membership of terrorist organisations. In Article 18(1), paragraph 1 is replaced by the following: 1 lit. b requested identification of customers by the financial institutions has already been complied with by the repeal of anonymity and by a change in the Financial Market Supervision Act a corresponding basis is to be created. It is also important to include institutions carrying out money transfers. I think this is a very important supervisory duty. In addition, there must also be changes to police laws, such as the Security Police Act, the Customs Act and the Customs Control Act. Since the time available to me is very short, I would just like to say something about the international standards. To the European Arrest Warrant: It must be clear that in the future in those countries that profess to do so, criminals will be transferred within 90 days. Austria has maintained the transitional period of five years. I do not see this entirely in line with our clear commitment to the fight against terrorism and crime. Secondly, the harmonisation of EU visas is an important concern. The bilateral negotiations that are needed between the United States of America and the European Union: We have to respect essential European standards against the United States of America and thus the following demands: no extradition in the event of death penalty – if a prisoner threatens the death penalty, he is not to be extradited – a normal court procedure must be ensured – no special jurisdiction must be able to execute a judgment – and fundamental and freedom rights must be respected – as an example of data protection here. In this sense, we will be happy to give our approval, taking into account these comments of the present proposal.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e13562	Federal Chancellor! Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! ==References== March 2002 begins a year crucial for the future of Europe [[Abg. ==References====External links== March!]] It is important to establish a new constitution for the enlarged European Union, a constitution to apply throughout the European Union. The Convention, which begins its work today, involves every state government, as the elaboration of a European constitution also includes an intergovernmental conference in which the Heads of State and Government represent the individual countries. The High House is also involved in the elaboration of a European Constitution, the National Council, which, together with the Federal Council for Austria, has the last word to speak on a future European Constitution. With our eight members or alternate members of the Convention meeting today in Brussels, we had on 21 December. A detailed debate on the objectives of a European Constitution was held on February, under the chairmanship of Federal Chancellor Dr. Schüssel and Vice-Cancellor Dr. Riess-Passer. With the Federal Government, we should start the dialogue today so that the High House, which will have to decide on the European Constitution, and the Federal Government, which will have to submit to us a corresponding treaty, can take the same line. In order for this process to begin in time for the Austrian future, which will last no longer than a year to develop a European constitution, Karl Schweitzer and I have asked this urgent question today: on a day when the question will be discussed throughout Europe as to what the European Union should look like in the future. Europe needs a constitution – we have explained this in our urgent request – but: What does this look like? [[Abg. May. Prammer: Oh, that's what you want to have answered today?- Oh, my God!]] Today, Mrs Prammer, we want to take up the debate on what our partner, the Federal Government, is working on a four-party resolution, in the Conference of Heads of State or Government, in the European Councils, in the Intergovernmental Conferences. From a frog perspective, some other question may appear much more urgent – for us, for the two applicant groups, however, in the interests of the citizens of our country, the question of the future of Europe and its constitution is an essential question. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Does Europe not already have a constitution? – Yes, there are a large number of contracts, but they are so unclear that even lawyers with teaching authority find out the respective legal status from these documents. "A monster equal" – "monstro simile, irregular aliquod corpus" – once called Samuel Pufendorf the Holy Roman Empire, which also had no written constitution. – Samuel Pufendorf, 17. 19th century! [[Inter-calls at the SPÖ.]] It is the same with Europe today: It is not really possible to open up the constitutional basis. This inadequacy has become very evident in the Treaty of Nice, where, for days, a reorganisation of the European Treaties has taken place in an Intergovernmental Conference, there has been no transparency and where, finally, as important questions have been decided as whether each country will be represented in every European institution, with which voting weights the individual countries will be represented in the institutions, as many members of Austria, for example, will have in the European Parliament. – This has triggered a process of frustration, and after that many parliamentarians have asked themselves the question: What is this Europe in which the basic constitution is agreed between the Heads of State or Government, in which Parliaments are not involved and where there is no transparent work in the preliminary work?- Oh, my God! – That’s what we want differently! It was the High House here that articulated and carried on with a four-party request: We want to talk about it! And we want to have a convention – a kind of "general states of Europe", a kind of "Paulskirchen Assembly" as in the Frankfurt Reich Assembly in 1848 – in which representatives from all the countries of Europe, from all parliaments in which the social partners come together to discuss in a transparent way this issue of the future constitution of Europe. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] That was, as I said, a four-party request. - And Austria has gone through it. Federal Chancellor Dr. At the Intergovernmental Conference in Nice, Schüssel has been committed to such a convention – and thus enforced. The creation of such a convention was then included in the final document in Nice. This Convention starts today with its work – but today our work must also start because we are finally those who will ultimately decide for Austria: Will there be a new European Constitution – or not? – I hope there will be such a one! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Ladies and gentlemen! In this urgent question to Federal Chancellor Dr. Schüssel, Karl Schweitzer and I, on behalf of our political groups, have asked some very important questions to the Austrian Chancellor, and we expect a positioning today, if possible, so that we can start this discussion process with the political groups and thus we will have a position to draw up at the end of this year, which will reflect as far as possible all the political groups in this House and which is also in agreement with the four representatives and the four substitute members of the European Convention, which only has 100 members. So that means that there are already four members there. Each group is represented in this Convention with a Member, so that we have the necessary information and rights of participation. What are these questions? – The first question is probably: What should such a constitution look like? Should this be a Constitution, as we know it from the Constitution – or should it continue to be a State Treaty, a State Treaty that does not provide itself for how it can be amended – that would be an overall change in the Constitution – but where Parliament continues to decide how the European Constitution comes about and how it is amended? This is the question of "competence competence", as the lawyers call it. That means it has to be clarified: Who finally decides whether the State or whether the European Union is responsible for resolving a matter? Whoever has the power to regulate this is sovereign. Today, these are the states, and I believe that in the future the states in Europe should continue to be sovereign. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] [[Abg. May. Schweitzer: Very right!]] The second important question, Mr Bundeskanzler, is the question of the future status of the Charter of Human Rights. We have all noted with great interest that at the Nice Summit, the Charter of Human Rights, drawn up in a kind of precursor to the Convention, was adopted as a non-binding Council recommendation. This is a comprehensive Charter of Human Rights, which includes not only bourgeois, political rights, but also cultural, social and economic rights and thus new ones. However, it is not binding. However, we have a binding European Convention on Human Rights, which can be filed and which has had an extremely positive effect on the protection of human rights in Europe. The question will be, Mr Bundeskanzler: How do we cope with the Charter of Human Rights? Do we want to include a relatively washed, comprehensive catalogue of human rights in the future European Constitution and then depend on the case law of the European Court of Justice, which then fills it with content, or do we want the European Convention of Human Rights to continue to be incorporated into the European Constitution with complainable, very detailed, very evaluated rights? This is a dispensation, and answers are needed. For us, Mr Bundeskanzler, it is also important to know the answer to the question: How will countries like Austria – I don't think Austria is a small country, I don't think it is a smaller country, but I think it is a weighty European country – how will our country be treated with other comparable countries in the future? Will there be a kind of predominance of the major countries, the larger countries such as Germany, France, Spain, Great Britain, Italy, Poland, or will countries like Austria be represented in all the institutions as today and protected in important questions by the principle of unanimity? These important questions are, for example, the following: Who decides about our water? Who decides on energy supply? Who decides on taxes? There is unanimity today. We would like to know, Mr Bundeskanzler, how the Federal Government sees it. We want to know how in future the countries comparable to Austria will be able to participate in the European Union. This is a very important question for us! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Another question is the question of closeness to the citizen. Just as we National Councillors in Vienna repeatedly in our constituencies with the questions: What are you doing in Vienna?, How are you doing?, What are you doing?, What are you getting your gage for?, so to a greater extent are the European Members confronted with such questions. They are also asked again and again: What are you doing? You're always in Europe, what are you doing? What are you doing for us? It is important for parliamentarianism in Europe that the European Parliament be strengthened into a full Parliament in its legal status, but that the European Parliament itself becomes more civil and more clear that the rights of the people are also substantially shared by the European Parliament. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Three other points, which are very important and which we have raised in our questions in the urgent question, concern the following questions: How, for example, can we transform the double track record in the European Union into a more effective, more understandable procedure in terms of foreign and security policy? What is the future of European foreign and security policy? Will this be incorporated into the Commission's competence? What will the Commission be like in the future? Will it become a European government, which is certainly in the interests of countries like Austria? How are the councils obtained? A Member of the Greens of Europe has referred to the Council of Ministers, in which the members of the government responsible for Parliament make decisions in specialised councils, as the dark hole of democracy. I ask you, Federal Chancellor: Are these councils a dark hole of democracy, or are these councils not exactly those bodies where the state parliaments are involved in European lawmaking about the ministers responsible for them? That is an essential question. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] Ladies and gentlemen! It is particularly important, however, that the tax-finding law is applied. Will it be so in the future that only Member States will be able to decide on taxes? And we know from the theory of the state that this is a key question: Who decides the taxes? At present, the European Union is not financed by direct taxes. I hope it will stay that way. But it would be important to hear from you, Mr Bundeskanzler, what we represent. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Ladies and gentlemen! We have therefore tabled this urgent request, because it is necessary for Europe to receive a new and transparent constitution that is understandable to all European states, a constitution in which smaller countries like Austria have powers of participation, a constitution that is closer to citizens and in which human rights have an important role.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e13731	I hope I can deal with the questions at this time too. First of all, I would like to thank Mr Klubobmann Khol for the opportunity to have a principle discussion here in the Austrian Parliament for the first time – on the same day that the European Convention in Brussels begins its work. I believe that we can all be proud that the idea of the Convention has actually taken its place from us, because we held the first small Convention in May 2001. In October, there was a four-party request, a request made by all the parliamentary groups, in which this idea was supported. At the time, it was not yet clear whether the other fourteen EU countries would accept this. We have then been able to achieve this in Laeken through a lot of lobbying and also with other allies. That is why, I believe, it is very reasonable that we are now considering what can come out of this Convention, accompanied by the national discussions. Andreas Khol! Congratulations on your open-minded discussion. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Unfortunately, I can't do this quite, but I'm going to try to emulate it. It said Samuel Huntington in 1988/89: If Europe unites politically, then this Europe could be in the 21st century. ==References====External links== The question is: Are we? Can we be that? How does reality look? Roger de Weck wrote a little skeptical about the European Union in the newspaper "Die Zeit": Everyone does what he wants, no one does what he should, but everyone does something. – That’s a little mocky, but it hits a little what we’re doing there. I believe that we now have the opportunity to make binding decisions as to how it could get better. It may even be the last chance, because once the Union has 25 or 27 members, it will be much harder to implement fundamental changes. I hold it with Wolfgang Schäuble, who said, without a binding clarification of what remains of decision-making powers for the Member States, a reform of the European institutions, whether Council, Parliament or Commission, will not be possible. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] It is now necessary to clarify what needs to be decided in Brussels or in Vienna or in other European capitals or in St. Pölten, Graz or Salzburg or Innsbruck, Klagenfurt or wherever. This question: Who decides what? is crucial. It is the three questions raised by Andreas Khol, namely the Union's ability to act, the equality of all Member States, the principle of democratic equality, but also the rights of citizens, those issues with which we now have to deal. Europe is, of course, an absolute global player from the economy: We produce almost a third of all the goods produced worldwide, we have 21 percent the highest share of world trade, we pay 57 percent of the development aid that is being provided worldwide, we provide 55 percent of total humanitarian aid, we carry 36 percent of the UN budget, and yet we do not act together, neither in the UN nor in the Monetary Fund, nor in the WTO, nor in other issues. I think that needs to change. The European Union's external representation must be improved, ladies and gentlemen. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] We have incredibly complex procedures and structures. Guy Verhofstadt, before taking over the presidency, gave a highly interesting presentation in Gottweig and once listed exactly what it looks like in the three pillars. In the first column there are regulations, directives and decisions – in English. Then there are recommendations and opinions that are not binding. In the second column there are principles and general guidelines, decisions on common strategies, decisions "tout court", joint actions and common positions. In the third column there are four types of action, namely common positions, framework decisions, decisions and conventions. The whole thing is now going through and can be crossed. We therefore come to a total of 33 to 34 different choices with the co-decision of the national parliaments anyway, but above all also of the European Parliament. - Friends, that's absurd! This is not understood by the citizen, who does not at all. Not even the actors in the European Council or in the European Parliament can really decide exactly in each case on the question: Is the first or the second pillar on it? It is much easier for other questions. This can and must be simplified. It's the same with the languages. Today it is spoken in 11 languages in the EU as a whole. There are already 4,000 interpreters there, and it costs 30 000 € for a day in a language when interpreting. With the enlargement of 10 countries, 10 new languages are added, because Cyprus also needs Turkish, because the northern part of Cyprus speaks Turkish and not Greek. This will create an additional complexity that will in fact be no longer manageable in the new Union. I therefore believe that there will have to be a change in the language regime, especially in the working languages, at the level below the Council, in the working groups, on various other topics. In the same way, it is on the question of the finances that I am going to talk to. On the second subject: Equality of all states. – This is a very important principle. If, as in the last few days, one conjures up the American federal constitution, one should also address the question of equal treatment such as the Länder, the Member States in the Senate. It cannot be that the European Union becomes an "animal farm" where "some are equal and some are more equal" is the principle. We all want to be the same! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Whether it is about the "blue letter" if the convergence criteria are not complied with, or about full involvement in foreign policy pre-decision processes, then everyone must sit at the table together and have a seat and a vote in all the institutions. I am saying this because, as a Foreign Minister in Amsterdam, I have been there and experienced that since Amsterdam to Nice and now in the run-up to the Convention, an enormous and bitter struggle is being waged for the repression of the influence of the small or a predominance of the large countries. Of course, it is sometimes a virtual debate, because much more often the big countries are disputing among themselves than the big ones and the little ones together. But I say quite openly: It must be a fundamental principle that all Member States are represented in all the institutions and that there is no one who can agree on important issues such as budget, constitutional changes, foreign policy issues, essential issues in the national interest. That seems to me to be very important. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] In other areas, we know that there will be more majority voting. After all, in 40 different sub-areas, the Nice Treaty has brought more majority decisions. It is essential that you also seek alliance partners, cooperation partners or coalition partners. This is also the basic idea behind the necessary "regional partnership" initiated by Benita Ferrero-Waldner. Like the Benelux countries or the Nordic countries, a central European platform will be of great importance here, especially if these countries already belong to the European Union. The third area concerns citizens' rights. I believe that we have underdeveloped rights of individual citizens. We have individual complaints in Austria, up to the Austrian Constitutional Court. Why should there not be a direct right of individual citizens up to the European Court of Justice in certain matters relating to European law and having direct relevance? Why should there not be a right of complaint to the Committee of the Regions on the question of the necessary subsidiarity or regional identity? Why should there not be the possibility for several national parliaments to refer a particular issue to the European Court of Justice? Why should there not be more European issues that we apply together and bring to the public? Why should there not be more exchange programmes, not only for students, where it works very well, but also for apprentices, for employees? Many variants would be imaginable, as would the anchoring of the rights to work, to language, to income, to education that could have a great significance within the European Community's own budget. Of course, the Charter of Fundamental Rights is imperfect, which is not at all a question, but I personally believe that the accession of Europe, the European Union, to the Convention on Human Rights, would have a very substantial significance and would therefore be of good quality to human rights in Europe. I will continue to represent this path! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] Now on the specific questions: The first question is as follows: How can we try to increase the guarantee of small and medium-sized countries' participation in the reform process? We can do this by voting together. I tried this when in the autumn of the Afghanistan crisis, in the course of the crisis after the 11th. In September, a so-called mini summit was held in front of Ghent, where only the three major states were present and to which another four states responded by calling. But the majority, namely eight states, was not there. At the time, I protested out loud against it, voted with the other smaller and medium-sized countries, which was of great importance, because both the Commission and the Presidency of the Council, but also British Prime Minister Tony Blair, assured themselves that they wanted to respect Community rules in the future. I also say very openly to you: I fully support what Paavo Lipponen said these days in the run-up to the Convention, namely that we will together ensure that the role and importance of the smaller and medium-sized countries is in any case preserved and not reduced. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Regarding the joint letter from Blair and Schröder to the EU Presidency, I can say: This is a text that can essentially be underlined. There are also little exciting things about it. That the European Council should focus on fewer points, that we should not have an overburdened agenda, that is not a question, but it is, I believe, on the Presidency and on ourselves to live this. The fact that the European Council is supposed to be more confidential than before is more a call to those who have so far sent information to the public. That is, I think, hardly to regulate any changes to the Treaty or legal basis. The fact that the Council, where it is legislative, is supposed to meet in public, I personally consider this to be an interesting proposal, which should be supported, as well as the idea of changing, streamlining and streamlining the number of specialist councils, is a long-standing Austrian idea. I believe that the General Council should be able to carry out its actual control function more effectively. The third question concerns the future role of the Council. I think we should distinguish exactly: What is legislative? What is executive? What is Community law? What is intergovernmentalism? One should also raise the question: Where is unanimity and where is multi-voice necessary? I say quite openly here: We are very interested in the fact that the role of the Commission is not undermined. She's got to stay strong! The large countries in particular often want to put some stumbling stone in the way of the Commission's efforts to put pressure on the decision-making process. I believe that we, the smaller and medium-sized countries, have every right and every wisdom that we fully support the Commission at every stage. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] Because this is the case, ladies and gentlemen, I would also like to take a massive stand against Johannes Voggenhuber, who, in an incredible statement of speech, stated that the "black hole of democracy" in Europe is the national governments. The democratic deficit has a name, that is the Council. Yes, please, I can say quite openly: Is the Council, are the representatives of Austria not fully involved in dialogue with national parliaments? Does anyone believe that if the decisions were to fall only more within the Commission without the Council acting legislatively and controllably, could the European Parliament replace the quality we have in the internal Austrian debate or have Denmark or Germany, where there are well-developed rights of national parliaments? I agree with each other where Parliament is strengthened at European level, where the Council is not weakened, but is involved as a partner and where the Commission plays a strong European role. If we do, we are well advised! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] The fourth question is a very important question, namely the question of the system of competence. – "Competence competence", which sounds complicated, the truth is that in recent years, based on some articles, especially on Article 308 of the European Treaty, practice has become more and more centralized, away from today's Community law. In addition, there is a case law of the European Court of Justice and the open method of coordination, so-called soft law, which systematically leads to a narrowing of national sovereignty. That may be quite all right in the individual case. Only: This is not always and not generally good. It is quite right that, in a particular case, in which we need a joint action by the European Union, we must also act, but we do not want to have an intrusion where, whenever it pleases, we can do practically everything, even if it is not about Community law. Professor Griller has found out that since the in-force step of the Amsterdam Treaty – which has not been in force for five months – it has been saying and writing 121 concrete measures based not on Community law but on the use of Article 308. That is why, I believe, the proposal from the Weidenfeld Group or the proposal from the Prime Minister Clement or the principle paper from Schäuble and Bocklet is that there should be a clear definition of competence in three blocks – exclusive Community law, mixed law and exclusive national competence – so significant, otherwise it will be more and more, as at a poor level, towards the estuary of national competences and sovereignty, and I do not think it is right. This is not in the interest of the European idea, ladies and gentlemen. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] The fifth question relates to those issues which are of particular interest to the public and which are not purely institutional. I believe that sustainability should be addressed in particular. Since Gothenburg, there has been a sustainability strategy of the European Union, and I believe that the European social model of an eco-social market economy, equipped with a sustainability strategy, is something that is precious and what is to be found in the European Treaties. We believe that, for example, the issue of transport is of great importance, which is of great importance to energy and nuclear safety, but also to consumer safety, food or whatever. There are indeed some very interesting suggestions in the agencies that are now being decided on for safety in the airspace, for food safety, for safety against organised crime and for safety in many other areas. By the way, I wrote a letter to President-in-Office of the Council today, José Maria Aznar, which says that my contribution to the Barcelona European Council will be that or that we are seeking a European area of transport and communication, because if we do nothing, then heavy traffic will increase by 50 percent over the next ten years. If you know that today a tonne of goods is transported on the train at the average speed of 18 km/h and on the motorway with a speed of 48 km/h, you know what can happen to us. This means that European regulations also urgently need to ensure that the citizens of Europe are not overwhelmed by these traffic blinds. I think that is very crucial! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] I therefore propose that the Commission propose very soon, without any delay, framework directives which provide for the principles of pricing, infrastructure use and also a fee structure which also allows for mutual exchange or cross-financing. The second issue is a single financial market, which takes account above all of the interests of small and medium-sized enterprises, which well supports different risks. Keywords: Basel II. I think this is one of the essential European issues to be discussed in Barcelona. Equally important is the issue of the European Employment Strategy – keyword: Compatibility of work and family – where family benefits, childcare facilities are to be evaluated in an equivalent manner in order to achieve progress for Europe and its workers. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] I propose a European area for research and development, because the gap to the USA is getting bigger and bigger. We already have a gap of about €76 billion in research expenditure today. The recent study says that US companies have 73 percent higher research expenditure than they do within the European Union, and in recent years, US companies have grown three times as fast as they do in Europe. We therefore urgently need this "European area of knowledge". These are contributions which, as I believe, are very important from the point of view of Austria and which, hopefully, will also stimulate the debate. The sixth question relates to the issue of transparency and proximity to citizens, the simplification of the Treaties. – I just want to answer this: I believe that the University of Florence has introduced a list of very interesting suggestions in this regard. I would like to point out: Whoever is interested in it, I will be happy to give this study or a summary of it. On the seventh question, to include the Charter of Fundamental Rights: I believe that legal commitment is an objective that we should not lose sight of in the debate on the future, but we also know that the Charter is not a perfect text. Some people today pretend to be. We are not satisfied with some wording, for example with the unclear provisions on the protection of minorities, which we do not think are impressive. If so, then a significant improvement would have to be made in the Convention. The alternative we have always considered is the accession of the Union to the Council of Europe Convention. The eighth question concerns the common efficient external representation. There it is that we have practically the versatility of a football team. On the one hand, we have the President-in-Office, the High Representative for the Common Foreign and Security Policy, the Commissioner for Foreign Affairs and the Heads of Government of certain Member States who want to take a stand and organise competitions against each other on the other. I do not think it makes sense for one, Solana, to have the attention and another, that is, Patten, to have the necessary financial resources. This should be brought together in the longer term. I call here a point very open, in which I feel a little different from my friend Andreas Khol, that is the question of finances. The question of fiscal sovereignty is a second question, and there should never be an increase in the tax burden for citizens, but if we want to be credible and, above all, effective in foreign policy, there must be another form of funding for foreign policy activities, whether this is in the Balkans, Afghanistan, in development cooperation or in other areas. Whenever the question arises: If the net payers lose and then win any others?, we will never come to a credible European foreign representation accepted by the media. I believe that this is a subject that should be discussed openly within the Convention. On the tenth question – this is about the cooperation of the Convention members with the Federal Government's Inner Austrian European Convention, as well as with the National and Federal Councils: I think this is a very crucial question if we want to play a role at all, because Austria is neither represented in the Bureau of the European Convention nor in the Secretariat of the European Convention. Therefore, a red and white-red team must be formed that formulates the essential concerns – with different accents or priorities, but nevertheless together. I believe that we must also have an interest in ensuring that national parliaments are not weakened in their participation. There are a lot of ideas. One question, for example, is whether one should not insist more strongly on informing the representatives of all European national parliaments to the same extent, not more in Austria, Denmark or Germany, much less in other parliaments. I think the same level of information would be useful. To this end, there should be joint debates between the committees of the European Parliament and the national parliaments. I believe that an exchange of views and, of course, the possibility of sharing experiences would often be very, very essential and could lead to a better view of the different topics. I therefore believe that we are doing well with the joint secretariat to provide the necessary information or topics to all members of the Convention of Austria, who are of course completely free and independent. I believe that it is also good to have an internal Austrian debate regularly in order to carry out the work in the Convention with the help of it. I also recognise that I have nominated as my representative Hannes Farnleitner, because it is also important that there is someone in the Convention who has experience with the Council meetings, who has worked for a long time in the ESC, the Social Committee, then has discussed many issues in the Council and the European Council and has also experienced the boundaries and opportunities, so that, of course, a wide range of knowledge can be brought together with the Austrian and European parliamentarians, which is truly well represented by Austria. I wish all Convention participants from Austria, the full members, the advisory members, a good success. I also wish them tenacity in the implementation of our – hopefully – common Austrian interests, and I hope that the Austrian public and the media will accompany this discussion actively and interestedly, as that alone counts. Europe is not a project of the elites somewhere in the ivory tower, but our common future, which we also want to shape together.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e14338	Mr President. Federal Chancellor! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! I would like to welcome the debate in Parliament on the important issue of the Convention. I acknowledge that we are talking about this, despite the two mistakes in beauty. The first one has already been mentioned in the early days. It is the fact that this debate is unfortunately taking place at a time when Parliament's representatives cannot be present here in the Convention because they are there. The second mistake in beauty is that this debate is taking place within the framework of a urgent question. I think this would have been a good opportunity to hold an EU debate, as it is planned, in the house where the parliamentarians of the government say what they want, where they even have the opportunity to order them or make a decision or make a recommendation that would probably have been in agreement, rather than doing so in the form of a urgent request, to the parliamentarians asking the government what they should actually do as freely elected parliamentarians. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and the Greens.]] In the words of Mr Schweitzer, I am no longer quite clear about the attitude at the government level. But I would like to leave the task of catching him here to the next speaker, Mr Spindelegger, and to deal with the content of this debate. [[Rejoicing with the Greens]] [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and the Greens.]] So much for Mr Khol and others to be right that the Convention will focus on legal matters, particularly constitutional issues, so it will be necessary not only to argue constitutionally in the debate and in the public conception of this project, but to show that this Convention is the opportunity to bring the European Union into line with the wishes of its citizens. In reality, this is the big task: The reforms necessary to ensure that the people of the European Union find themselves in the institutions, in the objectives, in the institutions and in the areas of work of this Union, their lives, their desires, their hopes. This is therefore a matter of content in the Convention, and this is also an institutional formal task. As far as content is concerned, namely the design of specific policy areas, social democracy is promoting the development of EU competences in many areas, i.e. the development of the Union's role in a political Union that is self-confident in this role in the world, without excluding other parts, especially developing areas in the world, from cooperation. So: Further development and preservation of the special European way of life and social security, further development of the Union as a peace project and at the same time international attitudes to the problems of the world as a whole. In particular, this means, of course, that we have to ask ourselves the question of fundamental and human rights. In this respect, I agree with what has been said so far. I am very grateful for the attitude and I also believe – and not only in my present role, I have already represented it – that accession to the ECHR would be the best solution and would also ensure that there would be no uneven development in these important issues within Europe – half of Europe will not be in the Union in the longer term – and that, where there is further development, all people of this continent could participate in it. The other areas are important: Employment, social security as an equivalent objective in addition to economic integration. In reality, we have seen some of the things that we have so far considered to be objectives or objectives only to be the achievement of instruments that we must first use in order to achieve the real objectives in Europe: Anchoring the objectives of growth and employment in addition to the objective of monetary stability, harmonisation of tax policy, creating a European competence in external trade, safety standards for CFCs, integrating the Euratom Treaty into the EU Treaty and eliminating the distorting special role of nuclear energy, further integration of foreign and security policy, defence policy – we believe – up to an independent European defence – and further development of the EU in the areas of justice, home affairs and consumer protection. What we are talking about is the reform of the institutions, i.e. the possibilities for citizens to participate in the life of the Union, is essentially the principles of the basic principle of democracy, that citizens should only be bound by those laws which they have adopted themselves or by their elected representatives. That is why we, as well as the other groups, are committed to an extension of the European Parliament. We agree that it will be a parliamentary assembly, with all parliamentary rights, and not on a standard like all other international parliamentary assemblies, which will actually be the development phase of the national parliaments on the change of 19-20. ==References====External links== We are in favour of maintaining the Council and closing the democratic gap in the areas of justice and home affairs, foreign and security policy. We are in favour of the creation of European direct democratic instruments such as referendum and popular desire, and we believe that the European Commission should be further developed and strengthened. Above all, we want to have a debate, as a democratic element could develop, proposing an election of the President of the Commission. Many of us believe or have meant that this could be done by the European Parliament. However, Alfred Gusenbauer's very interesting proposal has come to make it so that the strongest candidate group in the European elections occupies this function with the top candidate or a person. I believe that these are ways that are suitable to show citizens very clearly that they can really find themselves in the institutions and in the workings of the European Union. The debate on this is necessary, and it must not be limited to today's day. I hope that the willingness to deal with these questions will be present in a discourse, not only when it comes to just who is filling a certain afternoon here. It is about how we shape our future and the future of Europe.

[[Congratulations with the liberals and the ÖVP.]] In contrast to the multiplicity of understanding and also to the action of the Union institutions, the Community Treaties do not say that the future path of Europe must automatically lead to even more communion and to the state. On the contrary: We call for a clear division of tasks. The current tasks of the Union shall be critically clarified, reorganised and determined in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity, and identified in precise standards of competence and, if possible, incorporated into a contractual basis. Neither the return to the Union of any really necessary fields of action to the Member States nor the transfer of the one or other useful task to the EU should be a taboo. The principle applies to us: As much subsidiarity as possible. We want the European Confederation of States, not the state in which the small countries have to lose their identity and inevitably sink into insignificance. A single European State does not comply with the will of the majority of citizens of the Union, nor does it meet the requirements of an enlarged and therefore significantly heterogeneous Union. It only destroys the historically grown diversity. We are in favour of strengthening the control tasks of the European Parliament vis-à-vis bureaucracy. [[Congratulations with the liberals and representatives of the ÖVP.]] We see the Commissioners as top officials of the European Union and not as their ministers. We are – and this is very, very important – for maintaining veto rights, because only Veto remains the last way to defend very important interests and prevent developments that have serious negative consequences or consequences for our country. [[Congratulations with the liberals and representatives of the ÖVP.]] It is often criticized if you want to resort to this right, but I ask you, ladies and gentlemen: What is it to be found in the Treaty provisions of the Union if it is not possible, if it is necessary, to resort to them? We have nothing against Easter enlargement, but it must be tolerated for us, but also for the enlargement countries themselves. A hasty enlargement would also put too much pressure on their economy and lead to the sale of their economy and to a migration of workers in the border area that would not be good for both areas. A lot of construction work will be necessary to meet the economic conditions in these countries. Financial assistance can also be provided by the Community – it does. However, the previous pot for this must not be increased. It is necessary to redefine the funds away from the previous recipient countries. Finally, countries which claim to be euro mature cannot have the right to demand financial support in the long term. A realistic moment for the full membership of the new states is hardly seen six to eight years ago or perhaps ten years ago. The Union has recently gone a little hasty. It is important to neglect the pace and place more on the depth and order what has sometimes been pushed to the side. Europe should be based on the diversity and proximity of citizens, on the equality of the members and on their own responsibility. European policy is increasingly affecting the daily lives of citizens, so that it often retains the character of a European internal policy. However, there is an increasing question of whether this is necessary, justified or also targeting ever-increasing European policy in the living environment of citizens. The EU institutions and many so-called European politicians, despite their commitment to the principle of subsidiarity, often see every problem in Europe as a task for the EU. However, not everything that interests citizens and affects people in their daily lives legitimizes a uniform regulatory approach by the Union. It is important to think about it in this Convention.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e15320	Mr President. Federal Chancellor! High house! Our goal, ladies and gentlemen, must be a great and strong Europe, which meets its heritage, its responsibility for the future and finds the acceptance of its citizens. To this end, the European Union needs to be built up more democratic, closer to citizens and more transparent. It must focus on those tasks that can only be solved in a European way. This requires both the transfer of further responsibilities to the European Union and the transfer of current responsibilities from the European Union to the Member States. The ability to decide and act quickly in the European Union must be greatly improved. Acceptance for political decisions can only arise if citizens are clearly aware of who is responsible for what decisions. A clear definition of the competences of the Member States and the European Union is therefore the central task of this reform process and the key to its success. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] However, it is also necessary to reorganise the institutions and their procedures. So far, the European integration process has often developed according to the principle of integration wherever possible. This method has reached its limit. The time has now come for the relationship of unity and diversity to be arranged by a new distribution of tasks. In a Union of 27 and more Member States, which is long-term foreseeable, the economic, social and cultural differences will be considerably greater than in the area of the original six founding members. Central decisions will only be very limited in many areas of action of growing diversity. The Union must therefore focus on European core tasks and ensure its ability to act. The Treaty should also clarify that the principle of fidelity applies not only to the European Union, but also to the Member States vice versa, and that the European Union is obliged to take a gentle approach when exercising its responsibilities. In addition to the national identity of its members, it is intended to respect the regional identity of its members. All the basic rules, in particular those relating to the distribution of competences between the European Union and Member States, the financial constitution, the institutions of the European Union and their practices, are to be incorporated into the Charter of Fundamental Rights in a Constitutional Treaty. A Preamble will set out the basis, objectives and values of the European integration work. Leave I should like to mention one more policy area: asylum, refugee and immigration policy. The removal of internal borders within the European Union makes a Community approach in certain areas of asylum, visa and refugee policy and immigration policy indispensable. Asylum seekers, refugees and third-country nationals arriving for a limited period of time should have the same conditions for the reception, residence and termination of stay in all Member States. A uniform return practice for illegal immigrants and illegal residents in the EU helps to avoid abuse of the removal of internal borders. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] Finally, a Community approach can lead to a reduction in immigration pressure and better enforcement of burden sharing and solidarity within the European framework. Therefore, the call for a new EU competence for Community measures to regulate the distribution of burdens – the promotion of a balanced distribution of financial and numerical burdens, as set out in Article 63(a) of the Treaty. 1 point 2 is currently planned, it is certainly not enough – and the demand for clarification that the European Union has no competence to regulate the access of asylum seekers, refugees and immigrants to the labour market. Ladies and gentlemen! The Convention will have to do intensive and hard work in the coming months. I wish all his members in our best interests a great deal of success.

If a citizen turns to the European Commission and the European Commission shines through inactivity, then there is the possibility of calling on the appropriate body of the European Union to work. If this does not take place, one can bring an inactivity complaint. Or if a decision is expressed by the European Commission, for example, and I am not satisfied with it, I can refer a matter of annulment to the Court of First Instance. I do not think so much that our national rules, as the individual citizen comes to the Constitutional Court, should be a direct example, because we know – Mr Glawischnig will certainly give me the right to do so – that individual applications for the abolition of a law in Austria can be made, but that statistics are rejected 85 per cent, because the Constitutional Court finds in constant jurisdiction that it is necessary to proceed to the court of law, which is sometimes very costly and also takes a long time. It is therefore relatively difficult to deal with a citizen's individual concern to our Constitutional Court. The opposite is the case with, for example, the courts of the European Union, but also with the German Federal Court of Justice. Let me conclude by saying: I have said that if we are talking about a European constitution, we are also talking about human rights, and if we are talking about human rights, we are also talking about an acquis communautaire that is to be respected. It is only if this is respected that accession of new Member States is actually in question. [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] I believe that from today's point of view no one will go so far as to say that Turkey, which is undoubtedly not an aspiration to accession, could theoretically join. As there is, for example, the death penalty in Turkey, this would be completely unthinkable. But I cannot imagine that a country like the Czech Republic can join, which today still has the Beneš decrees in its legal system. [[Congratulations with the liberals and representatives of the ÖVP.]] [[Intrusion of the Abg. Dr. Wittmann.]] Beneš decrees were not only a reason for a general amnesty of murderers, but also the legal basis for the expropriation of generations. I think that's the problem. If you say here that the Beneš decrees are no longer valid, then you only need to look into the basic book, who is there in the basic book of the former SudetenGerman goods, if you can express it this way. That's where it says: The legal basis is the Beneš decrees from 1945.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e17284	Mr President. Mrs. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! What we decide here today is a breakthrough in the area of Austrian development policy and development cooperation. I would like to point out clearly what exactly we are deciding today. We decide a law that regulates which criteria and objectives we intend to use in future, in accordance with the means of Austrian development policy – at whatever level they are available. In the previous debate, it seemed as if we were going to adopt a budget law today. But that's just ahead. So the objectives were finally clearly defined, and as the top and most important thing in my view, poverty reduction is at the centre. I am pleased that, at least as far as objectives and criteria are concerned, there is also agreement with the opposition parties. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] I would also like to point out, however, that this catalogue of objectives – and I would like to say this in particular to Mrs Lunacek, because it also seems important to me – is very specific to the whole development policy of Austria, i.e. that this catalogue of objectives and criteria not only binds the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, but also binds other ministries, because the talk is about all – I may mention the law – "measures of the federal government... which are suitable for promoting the sustainable economic and social development of developing countries or for holding back the impairment of this development." I therefore believe that appropriate coherence will be enhanced, because that will have to be implemented. [[Abg. May. Lunacek: And who will control this?]] The Federal Minister for Finance already has an active working group together with the Ministry of Finance, i.e. cooperation is constantly getting better. As far as the additional funds are concerned, I would like to point out that the objective of reserveing 0.7% of the gross domestic product for development cooperation is one that we have committed ourselves to. Of course! For this reason, this law, in the explanatory remarks in general, and in particular, has also pointed out once again that, in achieving this objective, consideration should be given to development in the international community and in the EU. Within the EU, the need to achieve this objective has been reaffirmed once again, so it will be the common task of all of us to achieve this objective gradually. The 2003 budget will reserve 0.35% of GDP for development aid, part of it for debt decommissioning. Although bilateral development cooperation is also the most important thing for me, because it is directly the most effective part of development policy, we all have to say together that debt is also of great importance for developing countries themselves. As far as the position of NGOs is concerned, it has, in my view, been strengthened by this law, for they are expressly anchored in it, and on the other hand, it is necessary to point out that in hardly any other country such a large part of the funds for the bilateral development cooperation of NGOs and NGOs are being handled. And that will and should remain the same. I am very expressly grateful to the staff of the Ministry and, above all, to the Federal Minister Benita Ferrero-Waldner for how this law, which has been overdue for ten years, has been prepared and prepared in the parliamentary debate. It was not natural for the law to be widely discussed in the committee before the government template was prepared and to involve NGOs and AGEZ so often and so intensively. I am sure that the NGOs, for whom I would like to thank for their contribution, will also be found in this law, which for me, as what it is, is a description of how the means can be used, is a consistently successful law. Thank you.
