ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e17353	Mr President. High house! Since 1974, i.e. for 28 years, it has been possible for the first time to create a new law on development cooperation. For two years, we have worked intensively on the one hand in the Subcommittee for Development Cooperation and on the other hand with NGOs, especially with AGEZ and other relevant groups, in order to create a law corresponding to the new global situation. I mean, it's become a good law. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] I am allowed to repeat some of the positions that appear to me to be positive. First of all, the text of § 1 para. 5. In contrast to what you say, Mrs Lunacek, there is a clear legal provision that the federal government must take into account the objectives and principles of development policy in the policy areas to be pursued by it, which affect the developing countries. This is a fundamental and far-reaching provision that requires the entire federal government. Where is the best coordination body for this? – Of course in the Council of Ministers! So there is no need for an additional technical body, although there are small committees and informal coordination. So far, we have been trying to achieve coherence. I only recall two examples, the study fees for students from developing countries or for Everything but Arms, where we succeeded in bringing the position of development policy into line with coordination work. Secondly, the anchoring of the role of NGOs in the field of development, § 2 para. 4. In its bilateral development cooperation, hardly any other OECD country is going as far beyond the NGOs as we are, namely about half. It is not only the much expanded area of co-financing, but also, above all, the frequent form of direct supervision of NGOs. Furthermore, texting follows the AGEZ proposal to the most extent. The only difference from the AGEZ proposal is that the government template provides that "first and foremost" the capacities of developing countries are to be used. I ask you: What's bad about that? Faith Do you really want us to put the interests of the Austrian NGOs over those of the partner countries? Where, then, did the principle of ownership remain? Thirdly, The desire to anchor developmental education and cultural work – that is § 2 para. 3 – which was expressly expressed by AGEZ and also by you, my dear ladies and gentlemen of the opposition, I have deliberately responded. [[Call the SPÖ: That's the only point!]] Fourth: In addition, I have also taken into account a desire you have repeatedly expressed, because I have now foreseen that I will transmit the three-year programme to Parliament once a year, thereby also strengthening Parliament in the field of EZA. Finally, let me say something about the question, which is always referred to as the most important one from your point of view, namely the question of the 0.7% target. My knowledge is that there is no country in which the target of 0.7 percent is set by law. The figure of 0.19 percent for 2001 by Mrs Jäger and Mrs Lunacek today must be based on a misunderstanding, which is the minimum I can say, because the OECD figures are only known in July 2002. [[Abg. May. Lunacek: State Secretary Finz!]] He must have been wrong, or there must be something based on misunderstanding. These figures cannot be known before. Therefore, one can be serious, please, only based on the figures of the year 2000, in which Austria has achieved a figure of 0.23 percent of GDP, which is just above the OECD average. In the course of the preparations – if I now think of the future – for the Conference on Development Finance in Monterrey in Mexico from 18 to 22. The European Council held in Gothenburg and Laeken on March that tangible progress on the part of EU members – for example, the division – will be made to achieve the policy objective of 0.7 percent. And there, you know, Austria has informed the Commission that in 2003, we will achieve this EU average, which is currently 0.35%, which is also set out in the so-called Richelle report. By the way, I would like to say this again; that has already been mentioned by Mrs Hakl in the debate – generally confirming part of the explanations on this law that Austria is holding the policy objective of the performance of 0.7 percent, as formulated in 1970 in the framework of the UN. This statement is repeated once again in the Richelle report. Therefore, I must say again that I am very, very sorry that the opposition to this law refuses to consent, although we have worked so intensively with you here, although we have worked on proposals from AGEZ and although Iustitia et Pax has requested that we adopt this law unanimously. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e18811	Mr President. Mr State Secretary! High house! The new Financial Market Supervision Act adopted in the summer of 2001 should be adopted on 1 January 2001. ==References====External links== In the absence of the agreement of the major opposition party, this new financial market supervision could only be established as a legal entity of public law. Under one roof, securities supervision, banking and insurance supervision as well as pension fund supervision will be merged. In December 2001, the Constitutional Court now repealed part of the Securities Supervisory Act as unconstitutional. [[Abg. Hagenhofer: Again!]] This law would have transferred tasks of the Commercial Police and Economic Supervision, as well as the responsibility to impose the associated administrative penalties of the BWA. The tasks would thus be divided from the State administrative organisation and granted to a public body of its own. The Minister of Finance would only have had a limited management and supervisory power thanks to this construction. This violates the Constitution, as the Constitutional Court has now stated, and led to the repeal of certain provisions of this law. The partial collection is of great importance, since, as I pointed out earlier, the supervision of securities should be transferred to a general supervision of the financial markets in a few months. The rapid decision of the Constitutional Court allows the National Council to adapt the rules for the FMA in time to the Constitution. Dear ladies and gentlemen! Austria's new supervision of the financial market can now take place on 1st. As planned by the government, it is planned that the work will begin on April 2002 as a decision-free authority. A two-thirds majority is required for the status "non-wisdom" in the National Council. At the last moment, there was still a consensus of all parties. Among other things, the breakthrough brought more competence to the Austrian National Bank in the new supervisory system. Market and risk audits in the banking sector are now expected to take over OeNB employees. The obligation of the National Bank to carry out on-the-spot checks in the field of market and credit risk is now equally streamlined as the possibility of commissioning the OeNB with further examinations. A committee statement also indicates the scheme in the examination of financial conglomerates. It is also provided for by law that the OeNB is supervised with the credit and market risk assessment in a bank-dominated conglomerate. This will ensure that the existing resources of the OeNB in the area of banking supervision will also be used as best as possible for the supervision of the financial conglomerates. The implementation of these points has now also prompted SPÖ as the larger opposition party to give an O.K. to an insidious authority. This FMA is now a milestone for the Austrian capital market. It also complies with the international standards applicable to financial market supervision. These include, for example, the operational and budgetary independence of the supervisory authority. This independence is implemented in the best possible way by the freedom of guidance enshrined in the law. An operationally independent authority not least contributes to the stability of the financial market. At the same time, the interests of savers and creditors are also best protected. – Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e19003	Mr President. High house! It has been stressed several times today that today's legislative decision on a reorganisation of financial market supervision concludes the years of discussion of a necessary re-regulation. There have been two Court of Auditors reports, which, on the basis of current cases in the supervisory system, have indicated that there is a need for a change, that there is a fragmentation here, that the necessary forces cannot be pooled, that there have on the one hand been information gaps between the banking supervision and the central bank, and on the other hand, that in certain supervisory cases they have not always been properly coordinated. As a result of the new developments in the financial market, where insurance, banking and pension funds are increasingly growing together, it is in line with the international trend that a new regulation has become absolutely necessary. I would like to expressly thank you at the opposition for the really factual and constructive discussions. The aim of the scheme is to ensure that the new financial market supervision complements, where possible, its expert teams on the ground and, where possible, really cooperates with many on-site audits. On the other hand, of course, financial market supervision must also be given the possibility that it can still verify or understand additional audits and findings carried out by the National Bank. In particular, we consider this new regulation within the meaning of Basel II to be extremely important, because Basel II will bring about very significant changes in the money and credit sectors in the future. In this sense, thank you again. I think this is a good day for Parliament today. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e20042	Mr President. Mr. State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! Point Report by the Parliamentary Committee of Inquiry on the possible impact of election or election results by publication of opinion polls directly before elections or by the announcement of partial results before the official election end (1004 and to 1004 of the Supplements)
ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e21483	We arrive at the 18th. Agenda item. We are going directly into the debate. First of all, Mrs Mag. Kuntzl. I'll give it to her.
ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e21511	Ladies and gentlemen! In the first place, I would like to stress that the discussions in the Committee on Inquiry have taken place in a very factual climate, in a very factual atmosphere and that it has therefore been possible to reach a common outcome on which I would like to comment briefly. In the first area to be discussed by the Committee of Inquiry on the publication of opinion polls prior to elections, it was a question of whether legal restrictions are necessary in this context. We took the view that there is no need for action, on the one hand, from very basic considerations, because the question of freedom of the press is to be assessed here and, at least from our point of view, the protection of freedom of the press is a priority and we want to oppose every attempt to restrict freedom of the press wherever possible. Secondly, the question of the sensibleness of such a measure has also been raised because the fear of influence by opinion polls is, so to speak, very relative. In my opinion, the influence in this context is not caused by the publication of figures, but by the evaluation of these figures, on the one hand, in statements made by politicians, thereby creating a certain dynamic in the election campaign, and on the other hand by the introduction of comments from journalists who can also bring a corresponding dynamics into an election campaign in the last few days. These statements by us politicians and journalists cannot, of course, be prevented even if the numbers are not in the newspapers. You're not supposed to. Therefore, the question of sensibleness arises, and as a result of our assessment there is no need for action. What we have discussed very well, also with the experts who have spoken on the part of the media representatives there, is that it can make sense to reach voluntary agreements by the media representatives, that the media representatives voluntarily agree on self-regulatory rules on the publication of opinion polls. In addition, the joint recommendations of the Committee of Inquiry are that we recommend the media representatives to comply with such guidelines when publishing opinion polls before elections, which should facilitate the reader, the reader's evaluation, the interpretation of these data. Secondly, we recommend the establishment of a wise council to monitor the compliance with this agreement on a voluntary basis. The second area to be discussed by the Committee of Inquiry is the publication of high-income results and partial results prior to the vote on the day of election: It should be noted that, on our part, the discussion has led the debate that on the election day, all voters should be able to choose the same access to information on the basis of the same access. Although it is clear, of course, that more and more voters want to make strategic decisions, the voters or voters who choose in the early days and those who vote in the evening will nevertheless be able to decide on the same information options. It was therefore discussed whether it would be useful to use the data at the time of transmission or at the constituencies, at the numbering or at the uniform closing of the constituencies. The uniform closure of the constituencies would in itself be a fundamentally superior measure, but, on the other hand, there were significant objections from the media representatives, such as the fact that they could not then publish the accounts accordingly and that the printing media would have difficulties publishing the results the next day. Similarly, problems of the rural communities have been mentioned, so to speak, as regards the habit of choosing early, or the problems of the commuters, who then no longer have the opportunity to choose in their community in the evening. In the discussion, other political groups provided for a guarantee of these possibilities through the choice of letters. However, there are still fundamental reservations on the part of the Socialist Group against the introduction of the choice of letters. Therefore, the recommendations that the legal provisions are essentially sufficient for the data to be passed on to a limited circle until the end of the general election, and for the person who violates the agreement that results are not to be published in advance, to be deprived of access to the data. I think these are very good results of this committee of inquiry. Finally, I would also like to thank the many experts who have been available to us during the discussions in this committee of inquiry and who have contributed very significantly to this result through the much time and information they have given us.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e21926	The next speaker was Mr Krüger. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e21948	Mr President. Mr State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! This Commission had two issues on the subject, namely the possible influence of electoral behaviour by manipulative opinion polls, which will then be published before the elections. That was a problem area. The second problem concerned the possible impact of electoral behaviour on the electoral day by early publication of partial results directly on the electoral day. On the first issue, I – as it did not call for the opposition’s warning voices – took the view from the outset that, for me, the right to freedom of expression is superior to that very considerable right that decisions in democracy should be made in the fairest possible circumstances. I therefore believe that it is right that the Committee on Inquiry has come to the conclusion that there should be a self-restriction of opinion research institutes, namely that certain standards that must be observed may be created to indicate the sample, indicate the source, indicate the question and the like. But I am also in favour of the fact that those opinion research institutes that violate it are not torn in front of the Kadi. That is not a question for me either. The other question seems to me to be more serious, i.e. the influence of the early announcement of partial electoral results on the electoral day, for example of constituencies that already block at noon or at 1 p.m.. We see this again and again, we representatives of the parties know these results very early. [[Abg. Kiss: We from the country!]] There you can really see that these trends actually continue. I therefore believe that it is absolutely not desirable that these results be made known – now completely free on the Internet – and that the bandwagon effect of opinion research is triggered, namely that people are very happy with the winners. However, one will not be able to prevent that. The Internet is inexhaustible, and it would be completely obscene and inappropriate if one made bans here. The only thing that can be done here is to set a uniform closing time for the constituencies. I know that the communities and cities are opposed. I am absolutely of the opinion of the Ministry of the Interior; this says it would be appropriate for every municipality, every city, to determine the beginning of the elections itself, but to have a uniform end. Then there would be only 5 p.m. or 6 p.m. as the end time, and then there would be no early publications. On this occasion, let me say something about the practice I have learned as an election witness and a member of election commissions, and I hope that there is consensus within the House. It is about the so-called barbarians, which are widely used in the cities in particular. This is what I mean by: The Electoral Commission has representatives of the political parties. If someone gives his voice, then individual parties make so-called barbarians prepared by the parties. Those who have already chosen Mozartstraße 5 will be identified in it, for example, residents of a certain house. At 1 p.m. or 2 p.m., the helpful spirits come from the party, which not only bring the sausage semis and the drink – the tea or whatever – but who are also willing to accept this list. They then march into the party headquarters, and there begins calling. It starts the intervention. People will be called, and they will be told: You haven't chosen yet, and that's so important, comrades or whatever. I think that's wrong, I don't think that's right! The right to vote is a right – and not a duty. If anyone fails and doesn't want to give a voice, that's also his good right. I find it unreasonable that pressure will then be exerted on the people. That is why I am personally in favour of creating some kind of regulation here. There is a need for action in data protection that simply forbids it. It's not all right for you to say at 1 p.m.: You didn't choose, you liked it! – However, I would like to admit that this is often the opposite, because some of them are so angry when they have to go to the vote that they choose someone else. However, I believe that there should be a consensus here that this should be abolished.
ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e21988	Mr Bruckmann is next speaker. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-28-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00095_d7e22010	Mr President. High house! The Enquete Commission has dealt with two problems in exemplary cooperation. My predecessors have reported on the first – forecasts prepared before the election day. Therefore, if I may say so, for the sake of responsibility, I would like to comment on the second point, the announcement of partial results or high-end calculations before the end of the electoral process. It is not self- praise, but historical fact that it was I who put up the records not only in Austria but worldwide as the first. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Thank you! – In this respect, however, a new situation has occurred in Austria since the last National Council election, which was also the occasion for the establishment of this Committee of Inquiry. In the decades before, a gentleman’s agreement had sufficed that the media had brought first high-end calculations only immediately after the final constituency. In the age of PC and Internet, however, it can hardly be prevented that – incorrect or correct, accurate or less accurate – high-end calculations already find their way to the public during the election period. High house! To make what the Commission has put forward on proposals would significantly exceed the speaking time available to me. So let me say to Karl Farkas: Look It's up to you! – The 1004 report of the Supplements has been made available to you all. I myself was confronted with this problem for the first time in 1964 in the Lower Austrian country election, when I first attempted an unofficial high bill and, at 3 p.m., found with some horror that the final result for me was already determined, while the voters went to the polls for two hours. A personal supplement to which I have more permission than any other: A reform proposal cannot be found in the report, namely the one that simply fails to do away with accounting. Contingents are actually the most unnecessary thing there is; there was no one until 1966. But this wheel of history, Hohes Haus, will not be turned back.
